A more in-depth discussion in terms of sufficient statistics and Le Cam equivalence can be found in
[
27
]
.

Acknowledgements

We would like to thank the associate editor and two anonymous referees whose suggestions helped in improving the quality of the paper.

Appendix A
Representation theoretic tools

Our main tool in proving Theorem 3.3 will be the representation of the special unitary group
SU(d)
. We first define the representations of the group
GL(d)
and
S(n)
(the group of invertible matrices and permutations respectively) on
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
. Let
f_{\mathbf{a}}=f_{{\mathbf{a}_{1}}}\otimes\ldots\otimes f_{{\mathbf{a}_{n}}}
,where
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}
are basis elements of
\mathbb{C}^{d}
and
\mathbf{a}_{i}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
(note that
f_{\mathbf{a}}
’s span
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
) and consider the following actions:

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(T):f_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes f_{\mathbf{a}_{n}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow Tf_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes Tf_{\mathbf{a}_{n}},\quad T\in GL(d)

(33)

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}_{n}(\sigma):f_{\mathbf{a}_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes f_{\mathbf{a}_{n}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow f_{\sigma^{-1}(\mathbf{a}_{1})}\otimes\ldots\otimes f_{\sigma^{-1}(\mathbf{a}_{n})},\quad\sigma\in S(n).

(34)

It can be shown that the representation space can be decomposed into direct sum of subspaces each of which is a tensor product of irreducible representations of
GL(d)
and
S(n)
. This decomposition is called Schur-Weyl duality in the literature (for a detailed account see
[
19
,
21
]
).

(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}

(35)

Here the direct sum is finite and each
\lambda
corresponds to a
Young diagram
. A Young diagram is defined by a ordered tuple of integers
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d})
with
\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d}\geq 0
and can be represented graphically by a diagram with
d
lines each containing
\lambda_{i}
boxes. For example a typical Young diagram looks like:

with
\lambda=(3,2,2)
.
Also the operator
\pi_{n}(T)
can be decomposed as follows
:

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(T)=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\pi_{\lambda}(T)\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}

i.e the tensor representation of
T\in GL(d)
can be decomposed into blocks
\pi_{\lambda}(T)
acting on the irreducible representation
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
and these blocks have multiplicities equal to dimension of
\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}
. Thus we can decompose the tensor product of the states as follows

\displaystyle\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,n}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\tilde{\rho}_{\lambda}^{0,u,n}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}

(36)

\displaystyle\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,n}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\left(\rho^{0,u,n}_{\lambda}\otimes\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}}{\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})}\right)p_{n}^{\lambda}

(37)

\displaystyle U(\mathfrak{z}/\sqrt{n})^{\otimes n}\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,n}U^{*}(\mathfrak{z}/\sqrt{n})^{\otimes n}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\left(U_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{z}/\sqrt{n})\rho^{0,u,n}_{\lambda}U_{\lambda}^{*}(\mathfrak{z}/\sqrt{n})\otimes\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}}{\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})}\right)p_{n}^{\lambda}.

(38)

In equation (
36
) we have used the usual decomposition of the
\pi_{n}(T)
while in equation (
37
) we have adjusted the factors
p_{n}^{\lambda}
and
dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})
(later we give the exact expressions for these quantities) so that both
\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda}
and
\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}}{\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})}
are states (i.e. they are normalized). Equation (
38
) follows from the fact that
SU(d)
is a subgroup of
GL(d)
and its tensor representation has a similar decomposition.
Define

\Delta^{\mathfrak{z},n}(A)=U(\mathfrak{z}/\sqrt{n})AU^{*}(\mathfrak{z}/\sqrt{n})

and
\Delta^{\mathfrak{z},n}_{\lambda}
in a similar fashion. Then the last decomposition can be written in a compact fashion as

\rho^{\otimes n}_{\theta,n}=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\left(\Delta^{\mathfrak{z},n}_{\lambda}(\rho^{0,u,n}_{\lambda})\otimes\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}}{\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})}\right)p_{n}^{\lambda}.

Next we describe the subspaces
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
in detail. Let
\mathcal{A}(S(n))
denote the group algebra, i.e. the linear space spanned by elements of the form
\sum_{\sigma}c_{\sigma}\sigma
for complex numbers
c_{\sigma}
.
Also define multiplication between its elements in the usual way i.e

\displaystyle a=\sum_{\sigma\in S(n)}a_{\sigma}\sigma,\quad b=\sum_{\tau\in S(n)}b_{\sigma}\sigma

\displaystyle ab=\sum_{\sigma\in S(n)}\left(\sum_{\tau\in S(n)}a_{\sigma\tau^{-1}}b_{\tau}\right)\sigma.

Let
\lambda
be a Young diagram with
|\lambda|=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}=n
. We define two subgroups
\mathcal{R}_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}
of
S(n)
defined as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\{\sigma:\sigma\text{ leaves the rows of }\lambda\text{ unaltered }\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\{\sigma:\sigma\text{ leaves the columns of }\lambda\text{ unaltered }.\}

Now consider the action of
\tilde{\pi}_{n}(\sigma)
(henceforth we will denote this operator as
\sigma
) given in relation (
34
) and extend it to the group algebra. Next we define the following operators

p_{\lambda}=\sum_{\sigma\in R_{\lambda}}\sigma,\quad q_{\lambda}=\sum_{\sigma\in C_{\lambda}}sgn(\sigma)\sigma,\quad y_{\lambda}=q_{\lambda}p_{\lambda}.

It can be shown that the
\{y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{a}}\}
spans the subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
. Of course the elements in
\{y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{a}}\}
are not linearly independent. To describe a basis for
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
we need the concept of Young tableaux.

A Young tableau is a Young diagram filled with integers. A semistandard Young tableau is a Young diagram filled with numbers from
1
to
d
such that the numbers are non-decreasing in a row and strictly increasing in a column, for example:

1

2

2

4

4

is a semistandard Young tableau. Now if we fill a Young tableau with the integers
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{n}
along the rows then for every
f_{\mathbf{a}}
we get a corresponding Young tableau
t_{\mathbf{a}}
. As an example we associate the following vector with the semistandard Young tableau shown above -

f_{1}\otimes f_{1}\otimes f_{1}\otimes f_{2}\otimes f_{3}\otimes f_{4}\otimes f_{4}
.
Define the set

\mathfrak{A}=\{\mathbf{a}:t_{\mathbf{a}}\text{ a semistandard Young tableau }\}.

It can be shown that
\{y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{a}}\}_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathfrak{A}}
forms a basis of
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
.

An alternative notation (also used in
[
36
]
) is to use
\mathbf{m}=\{m_{i,j}\}_{1\leq i<j\leq d}
where
m_{i,j}
is the number of
j
’s in row
i
. Note that if
\mathbf{a}\in\mathfrak{A}
, then
\mathbf{m}
uniquely identifies
\mathbf{a}
. For the example above
m_{1,2}=m_{2,3}=m_{2,4}=m_{3,4}=1
and the rest
m_{i,j}
are
0
.

Now denote
|\mathbf{m}_{\lambda}\rangle=y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{m}}/||y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{m}}||
. Mapping these basis elements of
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
to the basis elements of the multimode Fock space will be a key element in constructing the channel.

From the definition of
p_{\lambda}
and
q_{\lambda}
it can be easily shown that

p_{\lambda}^{2}=(\prod_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}!)p_{\lambda},\quad q_{\lambda}^{2}=(\prod_{i=1}^{d}i^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}})q_{\lambda}

(39)

and consequently

\langle y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{a}}|y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{b}}\rangle=\langle p_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{a}}|q^{2}_{\lambda}p_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{b}}\rangle=(\prod_{i=1}^{d}i^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}})\langle p_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{a}}|y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{b}}\rangle.

(40)

We also consider the properties of the vector
|{\bf 0}_{\lambda}\rangle
(which is the
highest weight
vector
of the representation
(\pi_{\lambda},\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
)
corresponding to the semi-standard Young tableau where all the entries in row
i

are
i
. An immediate consequence is that

p_{\lambda}|f_{\bf 0}\rangle=(\prod_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}!)|f_{\bf 0}\rangle.

(41)

Moreover
\langle f_{\bf 0}|q_{\lambda}f_{\bf 0}\rangle=1
since any column permutation produces a vector orthogonal to
f_{\bf 0}
and thus the normalised vector is:

|{\bf 0}_{\lambda}\rangle=\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}!\sqrt{i^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}}}}y_{\lambda}|f_{\bf 0}\rangle.

(42)

The finite dimensional coherent states are defined as

\pi_{\lambda}(U)|{\bf 0}_{\lambda}\rangle
for
U\in SU(d)
. Since
[p_{\lambda},\pi_{\lambda}(U)]=0
and (
41
) holds, we get

p_{\lambda}\pi_{\lambda}(U)|f_{\bf 0}\rangle=(\prod_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}!)\pi_{\lambda}(U)|f_{\bf 0}\rangle
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\langle y_{\lambda}f_{\bf m}|y_{\lambda}\pi_{\lambda}(U)f_{\bf 0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle p_{\lambda}f_{\bf m}|q_{\lambda}^{2}p_{\lambda}\pi_{\lambda}(U)f_{\bf 0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle p_{\lambda}f_{\bf m}|q_{\lambda}^{2}\pi_{\lambda}(U)p_{\lambda}f_{\bf 0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{d}i^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}}\lambda_{i}!\langle p_{\lambda}f_{\bf m}|q_{\lambda}\pi_{\lambda}(U)f_{\bf 0}\rangle.

(43)

Appendix B
Bayes estimation of a one dimensional shift parameter

In this section, we adopt the framework for Gaussian states
\rho
in
[
32
]
to develop a Bayes result for estimation of
t
in the model
\left\{\mathfrak{N}(t\tau,\mathscr{S}),t\in\mathbb{R}\right\}
(cf. (19)) .
In
[
32
]
the characteristic function of
\rho
is given by

\phi_{\rho}\left(z\right)=\exp\left(i\theta\left(z\right)-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\left(z,z\right)\right)\text{, }z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

(44)

where
\alpha
is a positive symmetric form on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\theta

is a linear function on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
(cf.
[
32
]
, III.2.4). Here the
\rho
is a state on the Weyl algebra generated by the
Weyl unitaries

W\left(z\right)
,
z\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, which satisfy the canonical
commutation relations

W\left(z\right)W\left(w\right)=W\left(z+w\right)\exp\left(\frac{i}{2}\Delta\left(z,w\right)\right)

(45)

(cf.
[
32
]

, III.1.3) where
\Delta
is a bilinear antisymmetric (symplectic) form on

\mathbb{R}^{m}
. The forms
\alpha
and
\Delta
must satisfy the condition

\frac{1}{4}\Delta\left(z,w\right)^{2}\leq\alpha\left(z,z\right)\alpha\left(w,w\right)\text{, }z,w\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

(46)

This is equivalent to the following condition (cf.
[
33
]
, Theorem
5.5.1): for any
r
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}

\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the two
complex Hermitian matrices below are positive semidefinite:

\left(\alpha\left(z_{j},z_{k}\right)\pm\frac{i}{2}\Delta\left(z_{j},z_{k}\right)\right)_{j,k=1}^{r}\geq 0.

(47)

Suppose
\alpha
and
\Delta
are given by
m\times m
real matrices:

\alpha\left(z,w\right)=z^{T}\Sigma w\text{ where }\Sigma>0

(48)

(strictly positive definite) and

\Delta\left(z,w\right)=z^{T}Dw

where
D
is antisymmetric. The symplectic form
\Delta
is allowed to be
degenerate (cf.
[
32
]
, beginning of Chap III, §2), which means that
D

is allowed to be singular. Indeed
\Delta\equiv 0
, i.e.
D=0
is included as
the classical case, where
\rho
is identified with the
m
-variate normal
distribution
\mathcal{N}_{m}\left(\theta,\Sigma\right)
(here we identified the
linear form
\theta\left(z\right)
with a vector
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
). The framework of an antisymmetric, possibly singular
D
now allows to
include products of classical and quantum Gaussian states.

It is easy to see that (
47
) is equivalent to

\Sigma\pm\frac{i}{2}D\geq 0

(49)

i.e. the above complex Hermitian matrices are positive
semidefinite.

In
[
32
]
, III, §2, (2.1) it is further noted that (under some
conditions) one has
W\left(z\right)=\exp\left(iR\left(z\right)\right)
where
R\left(z\right)
is a self-adjoint operator (often
called a "field operator"), and from (
45
) it follows that

\left[R\left(z\right),R\left(w\right)\right]=-i\Delta\left(z,w\right)\;\mathbf{1}.

(the equality holding in appropriate domains). The
case
m=2
corresponds to

\Delta\left(z,w\right)=z^{T}Dw\text{ where }D=\Omega:=\left(\begin{array}[c]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)

(50)

which yields a one-mode Gaussian state. Assume
z=\left(1,0\right)
and
w=\left(0,1\right)
. Then
\Delta\left(z,w\right)=1
and

\left[R\left(\left(1,0\right)\right),R\left(\left(0,1\right)\right)\right]=-iz^{T}\Omega w\;\mathbf{1}=-i\;\mathbf{1}

and identifying
R\left(\left(1,0\right)\right)
with
P
and

R\left(\left(0,1\right)\right)
with
Q
, we obtain the standard
commutation relations

\left[Q,P\right]=i\;\mathbf{1.}

Then, since for field operators the mapping
z\rightarrow R\left(z\right)

is real-linear (cf.
[
47
]
), we can write
R\left(z\right)=z_{1}P+z_{2}Q
, and the Weyl unitaries take the form

W\left(z\right)=\exp\left(i\left(z_{1}P+z_{2}Q\right)\right).

It is well know that the characteristic function of the vacuum state in this
setting should be

\phi_{\rho}\left(z\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{\left\|z\right\|^{2}}{4}\right)\text{, }z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Comparing with (
44
), we see that
\alpha\left(z,z\right)=\left\|z\right\|^{2}/2
and hence the covariance matrix
\Sigma\
in
(
49
) should be

\Sigma=\frac{1}{2}I_{2}\text{.}

We can check condition (
49
) for this
\Sigma
and
D=\Omega
:
considering the equivalent condition (
46
), we have to
verify

\left(\frac{1}{2}z^{T}\Omega w\right)^{2}\leq z^{T}\Sigma z\;w^{T}\Sigma w

or

\left(z^{T}\Omega w\right)^{2}\leq\left\|z\right\|^{2}\left\|w\right\|^{2}.

Since
\Omega^{T}\Omega=I_{2}
, we have
\left\|\Omega w\right\|^{2}=\left\|w\right\|^{2}
, and setting
u=\Omega w
, the above inequality is equivalent to

\left(z^{T}u\right)^{2}\leq\left\|z\right\|^{2}\left\|u\right\|^{2},

i.e. the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, which becomes an equality for
u=z
. Hence
(
49
) is fulfilled for
\Sigma=I_{2}/2
, and for
multiples of the unit matrix,
1/2
is the minimal factor so that
\Sigma
can
be a covariance matrix. This agrees with the well-known minimum uncertainty
property of the vacuum state; the thermal states are then those with
covariance matrix
\gamma I_{2}
where
\gamma>1/2
.

It is now obvious that an
n
-mode zero mean Gaussian state which is a product
of
n
thermal (or vacuum) states is captured by the framework
(
44
) as follows:
m=2n
,

\alpha\left(z,z\right)=z^{T}\Sigma z\text{ where }\Sigma={\textstyle\bigoplus\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}}\gamma_{j}I_{2}\text{, }

where
\gamma_{j}\geq 1/2
,
j=1,\ldots,n
and

\Delta\left(z,w\right)=z^{T}Dw\text{ where }D={\textstyle\bigoplus\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}}\Omega

with
\Omega
from (
50
).

Since the form
\Delta
is allowed to be degenerate, we can also consider the
product of the above with a classical
d
-variate normal
distribution
\mathcal{N}_{d}\left(\mu,V\right)
by setting

m=2n+d
,

\displaystyle\alpha\left(z,z\right)

\displaystyle=z^{T}\Sigma z\text{ where }\Sigma=V\oplus{\textstyle\bigoplus\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}}\gamma_{j}I_{2}\text{,}

(51)

\displaystyle\text{ }\Delta\left(z,w\right)

\displaystyle=z^{T}Dw\text{ where }D=0_{d\times d}\oplus{\textstyle\bigoplus\nolimits_{j=1}^{n}}\Omega.

A non-zero mean
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is simply accounted for by setting

\theta\left(z\right)=\theta^{T}z
in (
44
).

In
[
32
]
, III.5 estimation of a one dimensional shift parameter
t

is considered, which has a normal prior distribution with zero mean and
variance
b
. The family of Gaussian states is given by (
44
)
where
\theta\left(z\right)
is a linear form on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
given by

\theta\left(z\right)=t\theta_{0}\left(z\right)
,
\theta_{0}\left(z\right)
being a fixed linear form and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume the linear form
\theta_{0}\left(z\right)
is given by a vector
\tau
such that
\theta_{0}\left(z\right)=\tau^{T}z
, then the family of Gaussian states
will have shift parameters
t\tau
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
and fixed covariance
function
\alpha\left(z,w\right).
Recall that according to
(
48
) we have
\alpha\left(z,w\right)=z^{T}\Sigma w

where
\Sigma>0
; thus, in this setup the characteristic function in (
44
) matches the characteristic function of
\mathfrak{N}(t\tau,\mathscr{S}),t\in\mathbb{R}.
Comparing (
51
) and (19), we note that
S=D
when
d=r-1
and
n=r(r-1)/2+r(d-r)
.

In
[
32
]
the linear form
\theta_{0}\left(z\right)
is represented
by a vector in a different way. First it is to be noted that in this section
in
[
32
]
the quadratic form
\alpha
has acquired a different meaning
from (
44
): the
\alpha
there is now written
\alpha_{0}
, and

\alpha
is now defined as (see (4.1) in III.4)

\alpha\left(z,w\right):=\alpha_{0}\left(z,w\right)+\beta\left(z,w\right)

where
\beta
is the covariance function of the prior distribution, defined
as

\beta\left(z,w\right)=\int\theta\left(z\right)\theta\left(w\right)\pi\left(d\theta\right)

where
\pi
is the prior distribution and
d\theta
represents integration
over linear forms (i.e. over vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
). In the present
case, the prior distribution is given by
\theta\left(z\right)=t\tau^{T}z

where
\tau
is fixed and
t\sim N\left(0,b\right)
. Hence

\beta\left(z,w\right)=E\left(tz^{T}\tau\right)\left(t\tau^{T}w\right)=Et^{2}\left(z^{T}\tau\tau^{T}w\right)=z^{T}\left(b\tau\tau^{T}\right)w.

So we have a matrix representation of the quadratic form
\alpha
:

\alpha\left(z,w\right)=z^{T}\Lambda w\text{, }\Lambda=\Sigma+b\tau\tau^{T}

where
\Lambda>0
since
\Sigma>0
.

Now the vector
\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
to represent the linear form

\theta_{0}\left(z\right)
is chosen such that

\theta_{0}\left(z\right)=\alpha\left(\theta_{0},z\right).

The relation to
\tau
is given by

\displaystyle\tau^{T}z

\displaystyle=\theta_{0}\left(z\right)=\theta_{0}^{T}\Lambda z\text{,
}z\in\mathbb{R}^{m},

\displaystyle\theta_{0}

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{-1}\tau.

The loss function for estimation of
t
is
g\left(\hat{t}-t\right)^{2}
for
some
g>0
; we can set
g=1
here. Then in
[
32
]
an optimal
measurement for estimating the parameter
t
is found, and the Bayes risk is
given by

E\left(\hat{t}-t\right)^{2}=\frac{ba_{0}}{a_{0}+b}

(52)

(after relation (5.3) of
[
32
]
), where
a_{0}=a-b
and
a=\alpha\left(\theta_{0},\theta_{0}\right)^{-1}
.
Note that we have used the condensed notation while stating the Bayes risk (see the discussion after the statement of Bayes problem in subsection 2.4).
In order to clarify the dependence of the Bayes risk on the parameters
\tau

and
\Sigma
of the Gaussian shift model, note that

\displaystyle a^{-1}

\displaystyle=\alpha\left(\theta_{0},\theta_{0}\right)=\tau^{T}\Lambda^{-1}\Lambda\Lambda^{-1}\tau=\tau^{T}\Lambda^{-1}\tau

\displaystyle=\tau^{T}\left(\Sigma+b\tau\tau^{T}\right)^{-1}\tau.

Define
v=\Sigma^{-1/2}\tau
, then

\tau^{T}\left(\Sigma+b\tau\tau^{T}\right)^{-1}\tau=v^{T}\left(I_{m}+bvv^{T}\right)^{-1}v.

Now defining a unit vector
e=v/\left\|v\right\|
we have

\displaystyle v^{T}\left(I_{m}+bvv^{T}\right)^{-1}v

\displaystyle=\left\|v\right\|^{2}e^{T}\left(I_{m}+\left\|v\right\|^{2}bee^{T}\right)^{-1}e

\displaystyle=\left\|v\right\|^{2}\left(1+\left\|v\right\|^{2}b\right)^{-1}.

Consequently

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\left\|v\right\|^{-2}\left(1+\left\|v\right\|^{2}b\right)=\left\|v\right\|^{-2}+b,

\displaystyle a_{0}

\displaystyle=a-b=\left\|v\right\|^{-2}=\left(\tau^{T}\Sigma^{-1}\tau\right)^{-1}.

The Bayes risk (
52
) now takes the form

E\left(\hat{t}-t\right)^{2}=\frac{\left(\tau^{T}\Sigma^{-1}\tau\right)^{-1}b}{\left(\tau^{T}\Sigma^{-1}\tau\right)^{-1}+b}.

(53)

Appendix C
Proof of the main theorems

C.1
Integral representation of Gaussian states

Recall the displacement operator
D(z)
(and the coherent state
D(z)|0\rangle
) defined in equation (7) and
D\left(\mathbf{z}\right)
(and the multimode coherent state
D\left(\mathbf{z}\right)\left|\mathbf{0}\right\rangle
) in equation (12). We adopt the notation

|z\rangle=D(z)|0\rangle,\quad|\mathbf{z}\rangle=D\left(\mathbf{z}\right)\left|\mathbf{0}\right\rangle

which will be the used in the following integral representation and subsequent proofs.

An alternate representation of the thermal state is given by

\phi_{\beta}=\frac{e^{\beta}-1}{\pi}\int\exp{-(e^{\beta}-1)|z|^{2}})|z\rangle\langle z|dz

i.e. a Gaussian mixture of
|z\rangle\langle z|
’s with the mixture distribution having mean
0
and covariance matrix
\frac{1}{2(e^{\beta}-1)}I_{2}
.
Similarly we have the following integral representation for shifted thermal states:

\phi^{z_{0}}_{\beta}=D(z_{0})\phi_{\beta}D^{*}(z_{0})=\frac{e^{\beta}-1}{\pi}\int\exp{-(e^{\beta}-1)|z|^{2}})D(z_{0})|z\rangle\langle z|D^{*}(z_{0})dz.

Generalizing to the multimodal case, the tensor product of shifted thermal states given in (16) can be written as:

\phi^{\mathbf{\mathfrak{z}}}=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq d}\frac{e^{\beta_{ij}}-1}{\pi}\int\exp\left(-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq d}\frac{e^{\beta_{ij}}-1}{\pi}|z_{ij}|^{2}\right)|\mathfrak{z}+\mathbf{z}\rangle\langle\mathfrak{z}+\mathbf{z}|d\mathbf{z}.

Similarly we have the following integral representation of the tensor product of shifted thermal states and shifted pure states given in (17):

\displaystyle\phi^{\mathbf{\mathfrak{z},r}}

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\phi_{\beta_{ij}}^{\mathfrak{z}_{ij}}\otimes\bigotimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r\\
r+1\leq j\leq d\end{subarray}}\phi_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{z}_{ij}}\quad\in\mathcal{T}_{1}(\mathcal{F}^{r})

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\frac{e^{\beta_{ij}}-1}{\pi}\int\exp\left(-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\frac{e^{\beta_{ij}}-1}{\pi}|z_{ij}|^{2}\right)|\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z}\rangle\langle\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z}|d\mathbf{z}\right)\otimes|\mathbf{\mathfrak{z}_{2}}\rangle\langle\mathbf{\mathfrak{z}_{2}}|.

(54)

Here
\mathfrak{z}_{1}=(\mathfrak{z}_{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq r}
is a vector in
\mathbb{C}^{r(r-1)/2}
and
\mathfrak{z}_{2}=(\mathfrak{z}_{ij})_{1\leq i\leq r,r+1\leq j\leq d}
is a vector in
\mathbb{C}^{r(d-r)}
. Note that we have used the notation
|\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z}\rangle
to denote the vector as an element of
\mathcal{F}^{r,1}
and
|\mathfrak{z}_{2}\rangle
as element of
\mathcal{F}^{r,2}
, where

\mathcal{F}^{r,1}:=\bigotimes_{1\leq i<j\leq r}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\quad\mathcal{F}^{r,2}:=\bigotimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r\\
r+1\leq j\leq d\end{subarray}}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Henceforth we will use this notation without mentioning the underlying spaces
\mathcal{F}^{r,1}
and
\mathcal{F}^{r,2}
when there is no chance of confusion. Following (10) we will define the number basis for
\mathcal{F}^{r},\mathcal{F}^{r,1},\mathcal{F}^{r,2}
as
|\mathbf{m}\rangle,|\mathbf{m}_{1}\rangle
and
|\mathbf{m}_{2}\rangle
, respectively. In particular, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbf{m}\rangle

\displaystyle=\otimes_{1\leq i\leq r,i<j\leq d}|m_{ij}\rangle,\quad\mathbf{m}=\{m_{ij}\in\mathbb{N}:1\leq i\leq r,i<j\leq d\}

\displaystyle|\mathbf{m}_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\otimes_{1\leq i<j\leq r}|m_{ij}\rangle,\quad\mathbf{m}_{1}=\{m_{ij}\in\mathbb{N}:1\leq i<j\leq r\}

\displaystyle|\mathbf{m}_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\otimes_{1\leq i\leq r,r+1\leq j\leq d}|m_{ij}\rangle,\quad\mathbf{m}_{2}=\{m_{ij}\in\mathbb{N}:1\leq i\leq r,r+1\leq j\leq d\}.

Proof of Theorem 3.3.

Construction of the channel
T^{r}_{n}

We will follow
[
36
]
to construct the channel. However we first state the redundancy of some representations in the low dimensional case. We will see that it is because of these redundancies that the mixture of pure and thermal states arise in the limit. In particular when stated for the low dimensional case some of the representations (those with Young diagram containing more than
r
rows) will vanish from the sum (
36
) or (
38
). Further the
m_{i,j}
s with
1\leq i<j\leq r
will determine the central state and the rotation, while the
m_{i,j}
s with
1\leq i\leq r
and
r+1\leq j\leq d
will contribute only to rotation. The former will correspond to the thermal states in the limit while the latter will give rise to pure states.

We start by noting that
f_{\mathbf{a}}=f_{{\mathbf{a}_{1}}}\otimes\ldots\otimes f_{{\mathbf{a}_{n}}}
is an eigenvector of
\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,n}

with eigenvalues
\prod_{i=1}^{d}(\mu^{u,n}_{i})^{m_{i}}
, where
\mu^{u,n}_{i}=\mu_{i}+n^{-1/2}u_{i}
and
m_{i}
is the number of times
i
occur in
(\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{n})
.
Since
{\mu^{u,n}_{i}}=0
for
i>r
we have

\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,r,n}|f_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\mu^{u,n}_{i})^{m_{i}}|f_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle

if
\mathbf{a}_{j}\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
for all
j=1,2,\ldots,n
and the RHS of the above equation is
0
if
\exists\text{ }j
with
\mathbf{a}_{j}>r
.

Note that
y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{a}}
is a linear combination of tensor products each containing the same multiplicities (i.e. the set
\{m_{i}\}
is same since
\sigma
just permutes the basis vectors) and that each tensor product has the same eigenvalue. Thus
y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{a}}
being a linear combination of eigenvectors (all of them having same eigenvalue), is itself an eignevector with the same eigenvalue. Thus we have

\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,r,n}y_{\lambda}|f_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\mu^{u,n}_{i})^{m_{i}}y_{\lambda}|f_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle

if
\mathbf{a}_{j}\in\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
for all
j=1,2,\ldots,n
and the RHS of the above equation is
0
if
\exists\text{ }j
with
\mathbf{a}_{j}>r
.

Recall that
\{y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{a}}\}_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathfrak{A}}
spans
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
. Now consider a semistandard tableau with more than
r
rows or that
\lambda_{r+1}>0
. By the definition, the entries of a column are strictly increasing and hence the
(r+1,1)
entry of the tableau is greater than
r
. Consequently there exists
j
such that
\mathbf{a}_{j}>r
and hence
y_{\lambda}f_{\mathbf{a}}
belongs to the null space of
\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,r,n}
.

Let
\Lambda_{1}
be the collection of Young diagrams with number of row less than equal to
r
i.e.
\Lambda_{1}=\{\lambda:\lambda_{k}=0\text{ for }k>r\}
. Consider the alternate notation given in the last paragraph Appendix A where one identifies
\mathbf{a}
with
\mathbf{m}
and denotes the normalized basis elements as
|\mathbf{m}_{\lambda}\rangle
. If
\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}
,
\mathbf{m}=\{m_{i,j}\}_{1\leq i\leq r,i<j\leq d}
since
i\leq r
for these
\lambda
s. Let
\mathcal{H}(\{m_{i}\})=\text{span }\{|\mathbf{l}_{\lambda}\rangle:l_{i}=m_{i}\}
i.e the span of the vectors
l_{\lambda}
with the same multiplicity
\{m_{i}\}
. Note that for each
\mathbf{m}
we have a set
\{m_{i}\}
but different
\mathbf{m}
can give rise to the same
\{m_{i}\}
. We now have a modified version of (
36
):

\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,r,n}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}\tilde{\rho}_{\lambda}^{0,u,r,n}\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}

(55)

with

\tilde{\rho}_{\lambda}^{0,u,r,n}=\sum_{\{m_{i}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\mu^{u,n}_{i})^{\lambda_{i}}\prod_{j=i+1}^{r}\left(\frac{\mu^{u,n}_{j}}{\mu^{u,n}_{i}}\right)^{m_{i,j}}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}^{\lambda}_{r}\cap\{m_{i}\}}|\bar{\mathbf{m}}_{\lambda}\rangle\langle\bar{\mathbf{m}}_{\lambda}|

where
\mathcal{M}^{\lambda}_{r}=\{m:m_{i,j}=0\text{ for }i>r\text{ or }j>r\}
and
|\bar{\mathbf{m}}_{\lambda}\rangle
s are orthogonal vectors which spans the space
\mathcal{H}(\{m_{i}\})
. We would like to write the above decomposition in terms of
|\mathbf{m}_{\lambda}\rangle\langle\mathbf{m}_{\lambda}|
but since they are not orthogonal we have to use
|\bar{\mathbf{m}}_{\lambda}\rangle\langle\bar{\mathbf{m}}_{\lambda}|
instead. Later we will prove that we can write the same decomposition in terms of
|\mathbf{m}_{\lambda}\rangle\langle\mathbf{m}_{\lambda}|
and an error term that goes to
0
. Note that although
|\mathbf{m}_{\lambda}\rangle
is a basis element for all
m
coming from a semistandard tableau,
|\mathbf{m}_{\lambda}\rangle
still belongs to the null space of
\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,n}
if
m_{i,j}>0
for some
j>r
.

We normalize
\tilde{\rho}_{\lambda}^{0,u,r,n}
so that

\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{N^{u,r,n}_{\lambda}}\sum_{\{m_{i}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\mu^{u,n}_{i})^{\lambda_{i}}\prod_{j=i+1}^{r}\left(\frac{\mu^{u,n}_{j}}{\mu^{u,n}_{i}}\right)^{m_{i,j}}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}^{\lambda}_{r}\cap\{m_{i}\}}|\bar{\mathbf{m}}_{\lambda}\rangle\langle\bar{\mathbf{m}}_{\lambda}|

(56)

where

N^{u,r,n}_{\lambda}=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{M}^{\lambda}_{r}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\mu^{u,n}_{i})^{\lambda_{i}}\prod_{j=i+1}^{r}\left(\frac{\mu^{u,n}_{j}}{\mu^{u,n}_{i}}\right)^{m_{i,j}}.

Finally we define
p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}=N^{u,r,n}_{\lambda}.\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})
(the dependence of
p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}
on
\theta
coming only from
u
) and we obtain the modified version of (
37
):

\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,r,n}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}\left(\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda}\otimes\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}}{\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})}\right)p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}.

Since
\mathrm{Tr}{\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,r,n}}=1
, taking trace on both sides of the above equation we obtain
\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}=1
. We also observe that because of the normalization both
\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda}
and
\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}}{\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})}
are states and so is their tensor product.
We have decomposed the tensor state
\rho^{\otimes n}_{0,u,n}
into blocks (each having trace 1 and thus a state) multiplied by a weight. All these weights are positive and add upto
1
and we can interpret this as decomposition into mixture of smaller dimensional states (original state was
dn
-dimensional). Similarly the tensored unitaries
U(\mathfrak{z}/\sqrt{n})^{\otimes n}
are decomposed into block matrices.

Defining

\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}(A)=U_{\lambda}^{r}(\mathfrak{z}/\sqrt{n})AU_{\lambda}^{r*}(\mathfrak{z}/\sqrt{n}),

we obtain the following equivalent of (
38
) in this modified setting

\rho^{\otimes n}_{\theta,r,n}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}\left(\Delta^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}_{\lambda}(\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda})\otimes\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}}{\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})}\right)p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}.

The channel that we construct will contain two parts - the classical part maps the weights or probabilities of the block to a normal distribution via a classical channel or a Markov kernel. The quantum part involves mapping the basis of each representation to the Fock basis of multimode state and the action of unitaries under this channel corresponds to action of the displacement operators. We now give a brief sketch of the channel mentioned above, the exact details of the terms involved deferred to later sections.

Define

T^{r}_{n}(\rho^{\otimes n}_{\theta,r,n})=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}(p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}\circ\tau^{r,n}_{\lambda})\otimes T^{r}_{\lambda}(\Delta^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}_{\lambda}(\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda}))

where
\tau^{r,n}_{\lambda}
is a Markov kernel defined below and
T^{r}_{\lambda}
is an map of the from
V^{r}_{\lambda}(.)V^{r*}_{\lambda}
from
M(\mathcal{H}_{\lambda})
to the multimode Fock space. The explicit from of the maps
V^{r}_{\lambda}
will be discussed later. We define
\tau^{r,n}_{\lambda}
as follows:

\tau^{r,n}_{\lambda}(A)=n^{(r-1)/2}\mu(A\cap A_{\lambda,r,n})

(57)

where both
A
and
A_{\lambda,r,n}
are sets in
\mathbb{R}^{r-1}
and
\mu
is the Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}^{r-1}
. Here

A_{\lambda,r,n}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{r-1}:|n^{1/2}x_{i}+n\mu_{i}-\lambda_{i}|\leq 1/2,1\leq i\leq r-1\}.

Also define
b_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}=p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}\circ\tau^{r,n}_{\lambda}
and
\Delta^{\mathfrak{z},n}_{\lambda}(\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda})=\rho_{\lambda}^{\theta,r,n}
. In this setup we have to show that

\sup_{\Theta_{n,r,\beta,\gamma}}||\bigoplus_{\lambda\in{\Lambda_{1}}}b_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}\otimes T^{r}_{\lambda}(\Delta^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}_{\lambda}(\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda}))-\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})\otimes\phi^{\mathfrak{z},r}||_{1}\rightarrow 0.

Define the following set of Young diagrams

\Lambda_{n,\alpha}:=\{\lambda:|\lambda_{i}-n\mu_{i}|\leq n^{\alpha},1\leq i\leq r\},\quad\text{ for }\alpha>1/2.

(58)

For convenience of notation we will denote
\Lambda_{n,\alpha}
by
\Lambda_{2}
and
\Theta_{n,r,\beta,\gamma}
by
\Theta
. For the displacement operator
D(\mathfrak{z})
we will denote the action
D(\mathfrak{z})(.)D^{*}(\mathfrak{z})
by
D^{\mathfrak{z}}
. Finally we note that
D(\mathbf{\mathfrak{z}})(\phi^{0,r})=\phi^{\mathbf{\mathfrak{z}},r}
. By triangle inequality one obtains

\displaystyle||\bigoplus_{\lambda\in{\Lambda_{1}}}b_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}\otimes T^{r}_{\lambda}(\rho^{\theta,r,n}_{\lambda})-\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})\otimes\phi^{\mathbf{\mathfrak{z},r}}||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}||T^{r}_{\lambda}(\rho^{\theta,r,n}_{\lambda})-\phi^{\mathbf{\mathfrak{z},r}}||_{1}||b_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}||_{1}+||\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}b_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}-\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle||\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}b_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}-\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})||_{1}+2\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda^{c}_{2}}p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}+\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||T^{r}_{\lambda}(\rho_{\lambda}^{\theta,r,,n})-\phi^{\mathbf{\mathfrak{z},r}}||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle||\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}b_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}-\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})||_{1}+2\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda^{c}_{2}}p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}+\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}(\phi^{0,r})-D^{{\mathfrak{z}}}(\phi^{0,r})||_{1}

\displaystyle+\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}T^{r}_{\lambda}(\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda})-T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}(\phi^{0,r})||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle||\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}b_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}-\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})||_{1}+2\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda^{c}_{2}}p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}+\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}-D^{\mathfrak{z}})(\phi^{0,r})||_{1}

\displaystyle+\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||T^{r}_{\lambda}(\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda})-\phi^{0,r}||_{1}

since
T^{r}_{\lambda}
and
\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}
are isometries. Using the integral form of
\phi^{0,r}
(we set
\mathfrak{z}=0
in (
54
)), we obtain

\displaystyle||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}-D^{{\mathfrak{z}}})(\phi^{0,r})||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\int f(\mathbf{z})||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}-D^{{\mathfrak{z}}})(|\mathbf{z}\rangle\langle\mathbf{z}|\otimes|\mathbf{0}_{2}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}_{2}|)||_{1}d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{||\mathbf{z}||>n^{\beta}}2f(\mathbf{z})d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle+\sup_{||\mathbf{z}||\leq n^{\beta}}||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}D^{z,0}-D^{(\mathfrak{z}_{1}+z,\mathfrak{z}_{2})})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}

where

f(\mathbf{z})=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\frac{e^{\beta_{ij}}-1}{\pi}\exp\left(-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\frac{e^{\beta_{ij}}-1}{\pi}|z_{ij}|^{2}\right)

and

\int_{||\mathbf{z}||>n^{\beta}}f(\mathbf{z})d\mathbf{z}=c_{1}\exp(-c_{2}n^{2\beta})

by usual concentration of a Gaussian. The second term can be written as:

\displaystyle||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}D^{\mathbf{z},0}-D^{\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z},\mathfrak{z}_{2}})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{(\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z},\mathfrak{z}_{2}),r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}-D^{(\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z},\mathfrak{z}_{2})})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}

\displaystyle+||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{(\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z},\mathfrak{z}_{2}),r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}-T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}

\displaystyle+||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}D^{\mathbf{z},0}-T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{(\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z},\mathfrak{z}_{2}),r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}-D^{(\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z},\mathfrak{z}_{2})})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}+||(\Delta_{\lambda}^{(\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z},\mathfrak{z}_{2}),r,n}-\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{z},r,n})T^{r*}_{\lambda}(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}

\displaystyle+||(T^{r*}_{\lambda}D^{\mathbf{z},0}-\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{(\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z},\mathfrak{z}_{2}),r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}-D^{(\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z},\mathfrak{z}_{2})})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}+||(\Delta_{\lambda}^{(\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\mathbf{z},\mathfrak{z}_{2}),r,n}-\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},n}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{z},r,n})T^{r*}_{\lambda}(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}

\displaystyle+||(D^{\mathbf{z},0}-T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}

In the above equations
\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{z},r,n}
is understood to be
\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}
, where
\mathfrak{z}=(\mathbf{z},0)
. The entire chain of inequalities can be summarized as follows:

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}||T_{n}(\rho^{\otimes n}_{\theta,n})-\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})\otimes\phi^{\mathfrak{z},r}||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}||\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}b_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}-\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})||_{1}\text{ (Approximation error of classical channel) }

\displaystyle+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}2\sum_{\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda^{c}_{2}}p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}\text{ (probability of extreme representations) }

\displaystyle+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||T^{r}_{\lambda}(\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda})-\phi^{0,r}||_{1}\text{ (comparison of unshifted states) }

\displaystyle+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta,||\mathbf{z}||\leq n^{\beta}}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{(\mathfrak{z}+\mathbf{z}),r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}-D^{\mathfrak{z}+\mathbf{z}})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}\text{ (comparing shift operators) }

\displaystyle+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta,||\mathbf{z}||\leq n^{\beta}}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||(D^{\mathbf{z},0}-T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}\text{ (comparing shift operators) }

\displaystyle+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta,||\mathbf{z}||\leq n^{\beta}}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||(\Delta_{\lambda}^{(\mathfrak{z}+\mathbf{z}),r,n}-\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{z},r,n})T^{r*}_{\lambda}(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}\text{ (noncomm. of fin. dim. shifts) }

\displaystyle+\int_{||\mathbf{z}||>n^{\beta}}f(\mathbf{z})d\mathbf{z}\text{ (concentration term) }.

The following lemmas deals with each of the above terms separately:

Lemma C.1
.

. For any given
\epsilon>0
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}||\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}b_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}-\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})||_{1}=O(n^{-1/4+\epsilon},n^{-1/2+\gamma}).

Lemma C.2
.

Recall that
\Lambda_{2}=\Lambda_{n,\alpha}
. Then for
\alpha-\gamma-1/2>0
we have

\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda^{c}_{2}}p_{\lambda}^{u,r,n}=O(n^{r^{2}}\exp(-n^{2\alpha-1}/2)).

Lemma C.3
.

For
0<\eta<2/9
we have

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||T^{r}_{\lambda}(\rho^{0,u,r,n}_{\lambda})-\phi^{0,r}||_{1}=O(n^{-1/2+\gamma+\eta},n^{9\eta-2/24})

Lemma C.4
.

Let
\epsilon>0
be such that
2\beta+\epsilon\leq\eta<2/9
, then we have

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta,||\mathbf{z}||\leq n^{\beta}}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||(T^{r}_{\lambda}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z}+\mathbf{z},r,n}T^{r*}_{\lambda}-D^{\mathfrak{z}+\mathbf{z}})(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}=R(n)

with

R^{2}(n)=O\left(n^{(9\eta-2)/12},n^{-1+2\beta+\eta},n^{-1/2+3\beta+2\epsilon},n^{-1+\alpha+2\beta},n^{-1+\alpha+\eta},n^{-1+3\eta},n^{-\epsilon}\right)

Lemma C.5
.

Assume the condition given in Lemma
C.4
, then the following result holds:

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta,||\mathbf{z}||\leq n^{\beta}}\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}||(\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z}+\mathbf{z},r,n}-\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathfrak{z},r,n}\Delta_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{z},r,n})T^{r*}_{\lambda}(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|)||_{1}=R(n)

Combining these lemmas we obtain

Lemma C.6
.

For
0<\gamma<1/4
and
0<\beta<1/9
there exists
\kappa>0
such that

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n,r,\beta,\gamma}}||\Phi^{\theta,r}-T^{r}_{n}(\rho^{\theta,r,n})||_{1}=O(n^{-\kappa})

Proof.

For
\epsilon>0
,
1/2+\gamma<\alpha<1/2
,
\eta<2/9
and
0<\beta<(\eta-\epsilon)/2

we have

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n,r,\beta,\gamma}}||\Phi^{\theta,r}-T^{r}_{n}(\rho^{\theta,r,n})||_{1}=O\left(n^{-1/4+\epsilon}+n^{-1/2+\gamma}+n^{-1/2+\gamma+\eta}+n^{9\eta-2/24}\right.\\
\left.+n^{-1/4+3\beta/2+\epsilon}+n^{-1/2+\alpha/2+\eta/2}+n^{-1/2+3\eta/2}+n^{-\epsilon/2}\right)

(59)

using Lemmas
C.1
through
C.5
. Note that we have omitted the error term in Lemma
C.2
since it decays expontially upto a polynomial term and hence is smaller than the rest of the errors. Also we have used the relations
n^{-1/2+\beta+\eta/2}=O(n^{-1/2+3\eta/2})
and
n^{-1/2+\alpha/2+\beta}=O(n^{-1/2+\alpha/2+\eta/2})
to gather the error terms. Now we can easily choose
\epsilon,\alpha
and
\eta
suitably so that the RHS of (
59
) is
O(n^{-\kappa})
for some
\kappa>0
.
∎

Construction of the reverse channel
S^{r}_{n}

We follow the proof given in
[
36
]
except that in our case we have a restricted class of Young diagrams.
Let
\sigma^{r,n}
be
defined by

\sigma^{r,n}:x\in\mathbb{R}^{r-1}\mapsto\delta_{\lambda_{x}}

(60)

where
\lambda_{x}
is the Young diagram such that
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}=n
,
and
|n^{1/2}x_{i}+n\mu_{i}-\lambda_{i}|<1/2
, for

1\leq i\leq r-1
. If no such diagram exists, we set
\lambda_{x}
to any admissible value, for example
(n,0,\dots,0)
.
One can easily show that
with (
57
),
\sigma^{r,n}\circ\tau^{r,n}\circ\sigma^{r,n}=\sigma^{r,n}
and for any probability distribution
on the
\lambda
which is in the
image of
\sigma^{r,n}
we have
\sigma^{r,n}\circ\tau^{r,n}(p^{u,r,n})=p^{u,r,n}
.
One then shows (see lemma 6.8 of
[
36
]
) that for any
\epsilon
,
we have

\sup_{\|u\|\leq n^{\gamma}}\left\|\sigma^{n}\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})-p^{u,r,n}\right\|_{1}=O\left(n^{-1/2+\epsilon},n^{-1/4+\gamma}\right).

(61)

The channel
S^{r}_{n}
(which is essentially the pseudo-inverse of
T^{r}_{n}
) is given as follows. Given a sample from the probability distribution
\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})
, we use the Markov kernel
\sigma^{r,n}
to produce a Young diagram
\lambda
. Conditional on
\lambda
we send the quantum part through the channel

S^{r}_{\lambda}:\phi\mapsto\tilde{S}^{r}_{\lambda}(\phi)\otimes\frac{{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}}{\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})}

with

\tilde{S}^{r}_{\lambda}:\phi\mapsto T^{r*}_{\lambda}\phi+(1-\mathrm{Tr}(T^{r*}_{\lambda}(\phi)))|{\bf 0}_{\lambda}\rangle\langle{\bf 0}_{\lambda}|.

For any density operator
\rho_{\lambda}
on the block

\lambda
, the operator
\tilde{S}^{r}_{\lambda}
reverts the action of

T^{r}_{\lambda}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{S}^{r}_{\lambda}T^{r}_{\lambda}(\rho_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=T^{r*}_{\lambda}T^{r}_{\lambda}(\rho_{\lambda})+(1-\mathrm{Tr}(T^{r*}_{\lambda}T^{r}_{\lambda}(\rho_{\lambda})))|{\bf 0}_{\lambda}\rangle\langle{\bf 0}_{\lambda}|

\displaystyle=\rho_{\lambda}+(1-\mathrm{Tr}(\rho_{\lambda}))|{\bf 0}_{\lambda}\rangle\langle{\bf 0}_{\lambda}|

\displaystyle=\rho_{\lambda}.

Now

S^{r}_{n}(\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})\otimes\phi^{\mathfrak{z},r})=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}[\sigma^{r,n}\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})](\lambda)\tilde{S}^{r}_{\lambda}(\phi^{\mathfrak{z},r})\otimes\frac{{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}}{\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})}.

and with the notation
\sigma^{r,n}\mathcal{N}^{u}_{\lambda}:=[\sigma^{r,n}\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu}))](\lambda)
and
q^{u,r,n}_{\lambda}:=\min(\sigma^{r,n}\mathcal{N}^{u}_{\lambda},p^{u,r,n}_{\lambda})

we have

\displaystyle S_{n}(\phi^{\mathfrak{z},r}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu}))-\rho^{\theta,r,n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}\left\{q^{u,r,n}_{\lambda}(\tilde{S}^{r}_{\lambda}(\phi^{\mathfrak{z},r})-\rho^{\theta,n}_{\lambda})+(\sigma^{r,n}\mathcal{N}^{u}_{\lambda}-q^{u,r,n}_{\lambda})\tilde{S}^{r}_{\lambda}(\phi^{\mathfrak{z},r})\right.

\displaystyle-\left.(p^{u,r,n}_{\lambda}-q^{u,r,n}_{\lambda})\rho^{\theta,r,n}_{\lambda}\right\}\otimes\frac{{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}}}{\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda})}.

Taking
L^{1}
norms, and using that all
\phi
’s and
\rho
’s have trace
1

and that channels (such as
\tilde{S}^{r}_{\lambda}
) are trace preserving, we
get the bound:

\displaystyle\left\|S^{r}_{n}(\phi^{\mathfrak{z},r}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu}))-\rho^{\theta,r,n}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}\left\|q^{u,r,n}_{\lambda}(\tilde{S}^{r}_{\lambda}(\phi^{\mathfrak{z},r})-\rho^{\theta,r,n}_{\lambda})\right\|_{1}+\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}}\left|\sigma^{r,n}\mathcal{N}^{u}_{\lambda}-p^{u,r,n}_{\lambda}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda^{c}_{2}}q^{u,r,n}_{\lambda}+\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}\left\|\tilde{S}^{r}_{\lambda}(\phi^{\mathfrak{z},r})-\rho^{\theta,n}_{\lambda}\right\|_{1}+\left\|\sigma^{r,n}\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})-p^{u,r,n}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda^{c}_{2}}p^{u,r,n}_{\lambda}+\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{1}\cap\Lambda_{2}}\left\|\phi^{\mathfrak{z},r}-T^{r}_{\lambda}(\rho^{\theta,n}_{\lambda})\right\|_{1}+\left\|\sigma^{n}\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})-p^{u,r,n}\right\|_{1}.

Now the first term is
O(n^{r^{2}}\exp(-n^{2\alpha-1}/2))
by Lemma
C.2
and the third term is treated in the relation (
61
). For the second term one uses Lemma
C.3
through
C.5
as in the proof of Lemma
C.6
. Thus we have the same bound for the reverse channel i.e.

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n,r,\beta,\gamma}}||S^{r}_{n}(\Phi^{\theta,r})-\rho^{\theta,r,n}||_{1}=O(n^{-\kappa})

(62)

∎

Proof of Theorem 5.1.

In the first step we use a
2
-design to construct a POVM which helps in constructing the least squares estimator. A
2
-design is a finite set of rank
1
projectors
\{|v_{i}\rangle\langle v_{i}|\}_{i=1}^{m}
which satisfies

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(|v_{i}\rangle\langle v_{i}|)^{\otimes 2}=\binom{d+1}{2}^{-1}P_{sym(2)}

where
P_{sym(2)}
is the projection operator onto the symmetric subspace of
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes 2}
. It can be easily shown by taking partial trace that
\{\frac{d}{m}|v_{i}\rangle\langle v_{i}|\}_{i=1}^{m}
forms a POVM. When the state
\rho
is measured using this POVM, we obtain outcomes from the set
\{1,\ldots,m\}
with the probability of observing
i
given by
\frac{d}{m}\langle v_{i}|\rho|v_{i}\rangle
. Now repeat this measurement on
n
states to get a set of outcomes
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\}
. Denote
n_{i}
to be the number of times
i
is observed and
f_{i}=\frac{n_{i}}{n}
- the relative frequency of observing
i
. The least squares estimator is defined as follows

\hat{L}_{n}=\argmin_{\rho}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(f_{i}-\frac{d}{m}\langle v_{i}|\rho|v_{i}\rangle\right)^{2}.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a linear map from
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
(the space of Hermitian operators from
\mathbb{C}^{d}
to
\mathbb{C}^{d}
) defined as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{C}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(X)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{m}\{\langle v_{i}|X|v_{i}\rangle\}_{i=1}^{m},\quad X\in\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{C}^{d})

The adjoint of
\mathcal{M}
can be easily defined as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}:

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{C}^{d})

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{*}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}u_{i}|v_{i}\rangle\langle v_{i}|,\quad u\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

Then the least squares problem can restated as

\hat{L}_{n}=\argmin_{\rho}||f-\mathcal{M}(\rho)||^{2}

where
f=\{f_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
. The closed form of the solution is well known and is given by

\hat{L}_{n}=(\mathcal{M}^{*}\mathcal{M})^{-1}\mathcal{M}^{*}(f).

Explicit computation yields (see
[
24
]
for details)

\hat{L}_{n}=(d+1)\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}|v_{i}\rangle\langle v_{i}|-I.

One uses matrix concentration inequalities to show that
\hat{L}_{n}
concentrates around
\rho
with high probability. Equation (6) in
[
24
]
gives the following rate of concentration

P[||\hat{L}_{n}-\rho||\geq\epsilon]\leq de^{-\frac{3n\epsilon^{2}}{16d}}

where
||.||
denotes the operator norm. However,
\hat{L}_{n}
may not be a density matrix although it has trace 1. One projects
\hat{L}_{n}
into the space of density matrices to obtain what is called a projected least squares estimator. However, as mentioned in the beginning we will take the route of
[
12
]
to project into the space of spectrally thresholded density matrices which would estimate the rank correctly. We define our estimator (called physical estimator in
[
12
]
) as follows

\tilde{\rho}_{n}=\argmin_{\sigma\in S(\epsilon)}||\sigma-\hat{L}_{n}||_{2}^{2}

where
S(\epsilon)
denotes the density matrices spectrally thresholded at
2\epsilon
i.e.

S(\epsilon)=\{\sigma:\sigma\text{ is a density matrix with eigenvalues }\lambda_{j}\in\{0\}\cup(2\epsilon,1],j=1,2,\ldots,d\}

We show that
\tilde{\rho}_{n}
concentrates around
\rho
and also satisfies the rank consistency condition.

The proof follows in the same way as
[
12
]
but we include it for the sake of completion.

Let
\hat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\hat{\lambda_{2}}\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{d}
denote the eigenvalues of
\hat{L}_{n}
. Let
k=0
and define
\tilde{\lambda}_{j}^{(0)}=\hat{\lambda}_{j}
for
j=1,2\ldots,d

for

k=1,2,\ldots,d

do

{

if

\tilde{\lambda}_{d-k+1}^{(k-1)}>2\epsilon

then

STOP

else

\tilde{\lambda}_{d-k+1}^{(k)}=0

\tilde{\lambda}_{j}^{(k)}=\tilde{\lambda}_{j}^{(k-1)}+\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{d-k+1}^{(k-1)}}{d-k}
for
j=1,2,\ldots,d-k

end

if

end

for

Note that if the algorithm stops at
k=\hat{k}
, then
\hat{r}=d-\hat{k}+1
is the rank of the estimator
\tilde{\rho}_{n}
and the eigenvalues are given by
\tilde{\lambda}_{j}=0
for
j>\hat{r}
and for
j\leq\hat{r}

\tilde{\lambda}_{j}=\hat{\lambda}_{j}+\frac{1}{\hat{r}}\sum_{j>\hat{r}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}.

In particular if
\hat{L}_{n}=\hat{U}_{n}\hat{D}_{n}\hat{U}^{*}_{n}
, then
\tilde{\rho}_{n}=\hat{U}_{n}\tilde{D}_{n}\hat{U}^{*}_{n}
where

\tilde{D}_{n}=diag(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\lambda}_{\hat{r}},0,\ldots,0).

Henceforth, we condition on the event
||\hat{L}_{n}-\rho||\leq\epsilon
which occurs with probability greater than
1-de^{-\frac{3n\epsilon^{2}}{16d}}
. By Weyl perturbation inequality (see
[
9
]
),
|\lambda_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}|\leq\epsilon
where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{r}>6\epsilon
(by assumption) and
\lambda_{r+1}=\ldots\lambda_{d}=0
. Now if
\hat{r}>r
, then for
j\leq\hat{r}
,

\displaystyle|\tilde{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j}|

\displaystyle=|\hat{\lambda}_{j}+\frac{1}{\hat{r}}\sum_{j>\hat{r}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j}|

\displaystyle\leq|\hat{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j}|+\frac{1}{\hat{r}}|1-\sum_{j\leq\hat{r}}\hat{\lambda}_{j}|

\displaystyle\leq|\hat{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j}|+\frac{1}{\hat{r}}\sum_{j\leq\hat{r}}|\lambda_{j}-\hat{\lambda}_{j}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon

Since
\lambda_{\hat{r}}=0
when
\hat{r}>r
, the above inequality implies
\tilde{\lambda}_{\hat{r}}\leq\lambda_{\hat{r}}+2\epsilon=2\epsilon
, which is a contradiction since
\hat{r}
was chosen so that
\tilde{\lambda}_{\hat{r}}>2\epsilon
.
Now consider the case
\hat{r}<r
, so that
\tilde{\lambda}_{r}=0

0=\tilde{\lambda}_{r}\geq\lambda_{r}-|\lambda_{r}-\tilde{\lambda}_{r}|\geq\lambda_{r}-|\lambda_{r}-\hat{\lambda}_{r}|-|\hat{\lambda}_{r}-\tilde{\lambda}_{r}|=\lambda_{r}-|\lambda_{r}-\hat{\lambda}_{r}|-|\hat{\lambda}_{r}|

(63)

Now consider the step
k
of the algorithm with
k=d-r+1
. Since
\tilde{\lambda}_{r}=\tilde{\lambda}^{(d-r+1)}_{r}=0
,
\tilde{\lambda}^{(d-r)}_{r}<2\epsilon
. A careful analysis of the algorithm shows that at after step
d-r
, the sum of the last
d-r
eigenvalues is distributed equally among the first
r
eigenvalues. Thus

\tilde{\lambda}^{(d-r)}_{r}=\hat{\lambda}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}(1-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\hat{\lambda}_{r})=\hat{\lambda}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\lambda_{r}-\hat{\lambda}_{r})<2\epsilon.

This implies that
\hat{\lambda}_{r}<2\epsilon+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}|\lambda_{r}-\hat{\lambda}_{r}|<3\epsilon

.Since by the condition of the theorem
\lambda_{r}>6\epsilon
, (
63
) leads to

0=\tilde{\lambda}_{r}>6\epsilon-\epsilon-3\epsilon=2\epsilon

a contradiction. Thus with probability at least
1-de^{-\frac{3n\epsilon^{2}}{16d}}
,
r=\hat{r}
.

To describe the concentration
\tilde{\rho}_{n}
, consider the following diagonalizations of the states

\rho=UDU^{*},\quad\hat{L}_{n}=\hat{U}_{n}\hat{D}_{n}\hat{U}_{n}^{*},\quad\tilde{\rho}_{n}=\hat{U}_{n}\tilde{D}_{n}\hat{U}_{n}^{*}

and estimate the error of the projected estimator as follows:

\displaystyle||\tilde{\rho}_{n}-\rho||_{2}

\displaystyle=||\hat{U}_{n}\tilde{D}_{n}\hat{U}_{n}^{*}-UDU^{*}||_{2}

\displaystyle\leq||\hat{U}_{n}\tilde{D}_{n}\hat{U}_{n}^{*}-\hat{U}_{n}D\hat{U}_{n}^{*}||_{2}+||\hat{U}_{n}D\hat{U}_{n}^{*}-UDU^{*}||_{2}

\displaystyle=||\tilde{D}_{n}-D||_{2}+||\hat{U}_{n}D\hat{U}_{n}^{*}-UDU^{*}||_{2}.

For the first norm one has the following bound

||\tilde{D}_{n}-D||_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{k\leq\hat{r}}(\tilde{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j})^{2}}\leq 2\epsilon\sqrt{r}.

For the second norm first we give an upper bound in terms of the operator norm

\displaystyle||\hat{U}_{n}D\hat{U}_{n}^{*}-UDU^{*}||

\displaystyle\leq||\hat{U}_{n}\hat{D}_{n}\hat{U}_{n}^{*}-\hat{U}_{n}D\hat{U}_{n}^{*}||+||\hat{U}_{n}\hat{D}_{n}\hat{U}_{n}^{*}-UDU||

\displaystyle=||\hat{D}_{n}-D||+||\hat{L}_{n}-\rho||\leq 2\epsilon.

Both
\hat{U}_{n}D\hat{U}_{n}^{*}
and
UDU^{*}
are rank
r
matrices, so rank of
\hat{U}_{n}D\hat{U}_{n}^{*}-UDU^{*}
is at most
2r
and hence
||\hat{U}_{n}D\hat{U}_{n}^{*}-UDU^{*}||_{2}\leq 2\epsilon\sqrt{2r}
. And thus we obtain

||\tilde{\rho}_{n}-\rho||_{2}^{2}\leq(2\sqrt{2}+2)^{2}r\epsilon^{2}\leq 25r\epsilon^{2}

with probability at least
1-de^{-\frac{3n\epsilon^{2}}{16d}}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem 5.2.

Upper Bound

With
Z\sim\mathcal{N}_{r-1}(u,V_{\mu})
, as the estimator
\hat{u}

the classical part of the risk is

R^{C}=E[\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}(u_{i}-Z_{i})^{2}+(\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}(u_{i}-Z_{i}))^{2}]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}(1-\mu_{i})

where we have used the relation
\mu_{r}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}\mu_{i}
.

We start to compute the risk for the quantum part by first verifying that we obtain normal random variables when we use the measurement
M
on the shifted thermal states. Denote
R_{t}=(-\infty,t_{1}]\times(-\infty,t_{2}]
. Let
X
denote the two dimensional random variable generated when using measurement
M
to estimate the state
\phi^{z_{0}}_{\beta}
. Then using the relation
P(X\in R_{t})=\mathrm{Tr}(\phi^{z_{0}}_{\beta}M(R_{t}))
we obtain,

P(X\in R_{t})=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\frac{e^{\beta}-1}{\pi}\int\exp{-(e^{\beta}-1)|z|^{2}})|z+z_{0}\rangle\langle z+z_{0}|dz\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{R_{t}}|\psi_{m}\rangle\langle\psi_{m}|dm\right).

Changing from complex to two dimensional reals in the second integrals by using
\mu=(\sqrt{2}Re(z),\\
\sqrt{2}Im(z))
and similarly obtaining
\mu_{0}
from
z_{0}
we have

\displaystyle P(X\in R_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\frac{e^{\beta}-1}{2\pi}\int\exp(-(e^{\beta}-1)||\mu||^{2}/2)|\psi_{\mu+\mu_{0}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mu+\mu_{0}}|d\mu\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{R_{t}}|\psi_{m}\rangle\langle\psi_{m}|dm\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\beta}-1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{R_{t}}\int\exp(-(e^{\beta}-1)||\mu||^{2}/2)|\langle\psi_{\mu+\mu_{0}}|\psi_{m}\rangle|^{2}d\mu dm

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\beta}-1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{R_{t}}\int\exp(-(e^{\beta}-1)||\mu||^{2}/2)\exp(-\frac{1}{2}||\mu+\mu_{0}-m||^{2})d\mu dm

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\beta}-1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{R_{t}}\int\exp(-\frac{1}{2}(e^{\beta}||\mu||^{2}-2\mu^{T}(m-\mu_{0})+||m-\mu_{0}||^{2}))d\mu dm

\displaystyle=\frac{1-e^{-\beta}}{2\pi}\int_{R_{t}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}(1-e^{-\beta})||m-\mu_{0}||^{2})dm.

where the third equality is obtained by using the expansion (7). Thus the generated random variable
X
is a bivariate random variable with mean
\mu_{0}
and variance
\frac{1}{1-e^{-\beta}}I_{2}=\frac{2\sigma^{2}_{\beta}+1}{2}I_{2}
(since
\sigma^{2}_{\beta}=\frac{\coth(\beta/2)}{2}
). A similar argument would yield that
M_{c}
(see Section 4) generates
cX
. As a special case when
\beta=\infty
,
\phi_{\infty}=|0\rangle\langle 0|
and thus the shifted thermal state at infinite temperature is just a shifted pure state. In this case the covariance matrix of
X
is
I_{2}
.

Now consider the quantum part i.e.

\phi^{\mathbf{\mathfrak{z}},r}=\bigotimes_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\phi_{\beta_{ij}}^{\mathfrak{z}_{ij}}\otimes\bigotimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r\\
r+1\leq j\leq d\end{subarray}}\phi_{\infty}^{\mathfrak{z}_{ij}}

and estimation of the parameter
\{\mathfrak{z}_{ij}\}
under the constraint that
|\mathfrak{z}_{ij}|\leq n^{\beta}
. Changing from complex to two dimensional reals (in this case write
\xi_{ij}=(\sqrt{2}Re(\mathfrak{z}_{ij}),\sqrt{2}Im(\mathfrak{z}_{ij}))
) we have the constraint set:

\mathcal{Z}=\{\{\xi_{ij}\}:|\xi_{ij}|\leq\sqrt{2}n^{\beta}\}.

Let
\kappa_{ij}=\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}
. Note that for the case
j\geq r+1
,
\sigma^{2}_{\beta_{ij}}=1/2
(since the state is pure or alternatively since
\beta_{ij}=\ln(\mu_{i}/\mu_{j})
and
\mu_{j}=0
implies that
\beta_{ij}=\infty
). Also reparametrize the Gaussian state
\phi^{\mathbf{\mathfrak{z}},r}
as
\phi^{\mathbf{\xi},r}
. The risk for estimation with the POVM
\bar{M}
where
\bar{M}=\bigotimes_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}M
is given by:

\displaystyle R^{Q}_{\bar{M}}

\displaystyle=\int\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{ij}||\xi_{ij}-\hat{\xi}_{ij}||^{2}Tr(\phi^{\mathbf{\xi},r}\bar{M}(d\hat{\xi}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{ij}(2\sigma_{\beta_{ij}}^{2}+1).

Thus when
\theta\in\Theta=\{|u_{i}|\leq n^{\gamma}\text{ }\forall i,|\mathfrak{z}_{kj}|\leq n^{\beta}\text{ }\forall 1\leq i\leq r,i<j\leq d\}
, the risk for the classical estimator
Z
and the POVM
\bar{M}
is given by

R_{Z,\bar{M}}(\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}(1-\mu_{i})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{ij}(2\sigma_{\beta_{ij}}^{2}+1).

(64)

Note that
2\sigma^{2}_{\beta_{ij}}+1=2(1-e^{-\beta_{ij}})^{-1}=2\mu_{i}/(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})
for
j\leq r
and
2\sigma^{2}_{\beta_{ij}}+1=2
for
j>r
. Plugging back the expression for
\kappa_{ij}
we obtain

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}R_{Z,\bar{M}}(\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}(1-\mu_{i})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}2\mu_{i}.

(65)

since the risk in (
64
) is independent of
\theta
and
n
.

Lower Bound

Consider the expression of the Bayes risk for estimating
\theta
under the following prior
\pi

\displaystyle u_{i}\sim

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(0,n^{2\gamma_{0}})\text{ }\forall 1\leq i\leq r-1

\displaystyle\xi_{ij}\sim

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{2}(0,n^{2\beta_{0}}I_{2})\text{ }\forall 1\leq i\leq r,i<j\leq d\

where the random variables are mutually independent and
\gamma_{0}<\gamma
and
\beta_{0}<\beta
. Again we change the notation from complex to the real i.e. we set
\xi_{ij}=(\sqrt{2}Re(\mathfrak{z}_{ij}),\sqrt{2}Im(\mathfrak{z}_{ij}))
. Since both the loss and the parameter space are separable into quantum and classical parts we introduce the following notations

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(u,\hat{u})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}(u_{i}-\hat{u}_{i})^{2}+(\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}(u_{i}-\hat{u}_{i}))^{2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\xi,\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j})||\xi_{ij}-\hat{\xi}_{ij}||^{2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle=\{u:|u_{i}|\leq n^{\gamma}\text{ }\forall 1\leq i\leq r-1\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle=\{\xi:|\xi_{ij}|\leq n^{\beta}\text{ }\forall 1\leq i\leq r,i<j\leq d\}

We denote
\pi_{1}
as the prior on
u
and
\pi_{2}
the prior on
\xi
and by the structure of the prior we have
\pi(d\theta)=\pi_{1}(du)\pi_{2}(d\xi)
.We give a lower bound to the maximum risk by the average risk as follows:

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}R_{\hat{u},M}(\theta)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Theta}(R_{\hat{u}}^{C}(\theta)+R^{Q}_{M}(\theta))d\pi(\theta)

\displaystyle\geq\pi_{2}(\mathcal{Z})\inf_{\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}}\int_{\mathcal{U}}E_{u}[\mathcal{L}(u,\hat{u})]d\pi_{1}(u)+\pi_{1}(\mathcal{U})\inf_{M:M(\mathcal{Z}^{c}=0)}\int_{\mathcal{Z}}E_{\xi}[\mathcal{L}(\xi,\hat{\xi})]d\pi_{2}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}(\mathcal{Z})\inf_{\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}}(\int E_{u}[\mathcal{L}(u,\hat{u})]d\pi_{1}(u)-\int_{\mathcal{U}^{c}}E_{u}[\mathcal{L}(u,\hat{u})]d\pi_{1}(u))

\displaystyle+\pi_{1}(\mathcal{U})\inf_{M:M(\mathcal{Z}^{c}=0)}(\int E_{\xi}[\mathcal{L}(\xi,\hat{\xi})]d\pi_{2}(\xi)-\int_{\mathcal{Z}^{c}}E_{\xi}[\mathcal{L}(\xi,\hat{\xi})]d\pi_{2}(\xi)).

(66)

Since the parameters lie in
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{Z}
respectively it is enough to consider estimators supported on the parameter space themselves and hence the second inequality follows.

Let
Y\sim\mathcal{N}_{d}(u,\Sigma_{1})
and
u\sim\mathcal{N}_{d}(0,\Sigma_{2})
, then
E[u|Y]
is known to be the Bayes estimator of
u
under the loss
||u-\hat{u}||^{2}
. It can be easily shown that
E[u|Y]=(I+\Sigma_{1}\Sigma^{-1}_{2})^{-1}Y
and the Bayes risk is given by

E[||u-E[u|Y]||^{2}]=\mathrm{Tr}((\Sigma^{-1}_{1}+\Sigma^{-1}_{2})^{-1}).

Using the above result we observe that

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}}\int E_{u}[\mathcal{L}(u,\hat{u})]d\pi_{1}(u)\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{u}}\left(\int E_{u}[\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}(u_{i}-\hat{u}_{i})^{2}]d\pi_{1}(u)+\int E_{u}[(\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}(u_{i}-\hat{u}_{i}))^{2}]d\pi_{1}(u)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\hat{u}}\int E_{u}[\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}(u_{i}-\hat{u}_{i})^{2}]d\pi_{1}(u)+\inf_{w}\int E_{u}[(\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}u_{i}-w)^{2}]d\pi_{1}(u)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}((\Sigma_{1}^{-1}+\Sigma_{2}^{-1})^{-1})+\frac{\sigma_{r}^{2}\tau_{r}^{2}}{\sigma_{r}^{2}+\tau_{r}^{2}}.

Here
\Sigma_{1}=V(\mu)
,
\Sigma_{2}=n^{2\gamma_{0}}I_{r-1}
,
\tau_{r}^{2}=(r-1)n^{2\gamma_{0}}
and

\sigma_{r}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}\mu_{i}(1-\mu_{i})-\sum_{i\neq j}\mu_{i}\mu_{j}=\mu_{r}(1-\mu_{r}).

Taking limit one obtains

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}}\int E_{u}[\mathcal{L}(u,\hat{u})]d\pi_{1}(u)\geq\mathrm{Tr}\;V(\mu)+\sigma_{r}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}(1-\mu_{i}).

(67)

For the quantum part the Bayes risk for a single component
\xi_{ij}
with the prior
\xi_{ij}\sim\sigma^{2}I_{2}
is obtained by the covariant measurement followed by an appropriate shrinkage (see section 4 for details) and the Bayes risk is given by

\inf_{M}\int\int||\xi_{ij}-\hat{\xi_{ij}}||^{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\phi^{\xi_{ij}}_{\beta_{ij}}M(d\hat{\xi_{ij}})\right)d\pi(\xi_{ij})=\frac{2\sigma^{2}(2\sigma^{2}_{\beta_{ij}}+1)}{2(\sigma^{2}+\sigma^{2}_{\beta_{ij}})+1}

which is essentially equation (28). Thus we have

\displaystyle\inf_{M:M(\mathcal{Z}^{c}=0)}\int E_{\xi}[\mathcal{L}(\xi,\hat{\xi})]d\pi_{2}(\xi)\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{M}\int(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{ij}||\xi_{ij}-\hat{\xi_{ij}}||^{2}\mathrm{Tr}[\phi^{\xi_{ij}}_{\beta_{ij}}M(d\hat{\xi_{ij}})])d\pi_{2}(\xi)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{ij}\frac{2n^{2\beta_{0}}(2\sigma^{2}_{\beta_{ij}}+1)}{2(n^{2\beta_{0}}+\sigma^{2}_{\beta_{ij}})+1}.

Taking limit we obtain

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{M:M(\mathcal{Z}^{c}=0)}\int E_{\xi}[\mathcal{L}(\xi,\hat{\xi})]d\pi_{2}(\xi)\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{ij}(2\sigma^{2}_{\beta_{ij}}+1)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}2\mu_{i}

(68)

the last equality following from the calculation preceding (
65
). We also have the following lemma (the proof of which is deferred to Appendix D) which shows that the second and fourth term of (
66
) goes to zero.

Lemma C.7
.

Let
\hat{u}
be an estimator of
u
and suppose
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{U}
. Similarly suppose we use the POVM
M
to estimate
\xi
where
M(\mathcal{Z}^{c})=0
. Then we have

\int_{\mathcal{U}^{c}}E_{u}[\mathcal{L}(u,\hat{u})]d\pi_{1}(u),\int_{\mathcal{Z}^{c}}E_{\xi}[\mathcal{L}(\xi,\hat{\xi})]d\pi_{2}(\xi)\rightarrow 0.

Finally we observe that
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{U}),\pi_{2}(\mathcal{Z})\rightarrow 1
, which together with (
67
),(
68
) and Lemma
C.7
yields

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{\hat{u},M}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}R_{\hat{u},M}(\theta)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}(1-\mu_{i})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}2\mu_{i}

when the terms are plugged in (
66
).
∎

Proof of Theorem 5.4.

Upper Bound

Split the sample into two parts
n_{1}=\lfloor n^{\delta_{0}}\rfloor+1
for some
0<\delta_{0}<1
(we will describe the choice of
\delta_{0}
in the next step) and
n_{2}=n-n_{1}
. Let
\tilde{\rho}_{n}
be the estimator obtained in the first stage with the the sample
n_{1}
. Set
25r\epsilon^{2}=n^{-1+2\epsilon_{0}}
(where
\epsilon_{0}<\gamma
). By Theorem 5.1

P[||\rho-\tilde{\rho}_{n}||^{2}_{2}\geq n^{-1+2\epsilon_{0}}]\leq de^{-3n^{-1+2\epsilon_{0}+\delta_{0}}/400rd}.

(69)

since we have obtained
\tilde{\rho}_{n}
using
n_{1}>n^{\delta_{0}}
. For any
\epsilon_{0}
we can choose
\delta_{0}
such that
2\epsilon_{0}+\delta_{0}>1
and the concentration still holds.
Further we have

P[r=\hat{r}]\geq 1-de^{-3n^{-1+2\epsilon_{0}+\delta_{0}}/400rd}.

Now for the event
A_{n}=\{||\rho-\tilde{\rho}_{n}||^{2}_{2}<n^{-1+2\epsilon_{0}}\text{ and }r=\hat{r}\}
we have,

P[A_{n}]=1-2de^{-3n^{-1+2\epsilon_{0}+\delta_{0}}/400rd}.

(70)

Recall the definition
\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta})
given in (31). The risk can now be decomposed as follows

\displaystyle E_{\rho}||\rho-\hat{\rho}_{n}||_{2}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle 4P[A^{c}_{n}]+E_{\rho}[||\rho-\hat{\rho}_{n}||^{2}\mathds{1}_{A_{n}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4P[A^{c}_{n}]+E_{\rho_{\theta}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta}_{n})+O\left(\frac{||\theta||^{3},||\hat{\theta}_{n}||^{3}}{n^{3/2}}\right)\right)\mathds{1}_{A_{n}}\right].

We have used the notation involving local parameters in the last line with
\rho_{0}=\tilde{\rho}_{n}
as the central state. Also the local parameter estimate
\hat{\theta}_{n}
(and the corresponding state
\hat{\rho}_{n}
) is formed using the second part of the sample
n_{2}
. Conditioned on
A_{n}
,
||\theta||<n^{\epsilon_{0}}
and we can restrict
\hat{\theta}_{n}
to lie within a radius of
2n^{\epsilon_{0}}
. This ensures that
||\theta||<n^{\epsilon_{0}}<n^{\gamma}
(choose
\beta<\gamma<1/9
) we can use LAN. Secondly both
||\theta||^{3}
and
||\hat{\theta}_{n}||^{3}
are less than
n^{3\gamma}<n^{1/2-\epsilon_{1}}
for a sufficiently small
\epsilon_{1}
so that
O(\frac{||\theta||^{3},||\hat{\theta}_{n}||^{3}}{n^{3/2}})=o(n^{-1})
. Thus we obtain

E_{\rho}n||\rho-\hat{\rho}_{n}||_{2}^{2}\leq 4nP[A^{c}_{n}]+E_{\rho_{\theta}}\left[\left(\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta})\right)\mathds{1}_{A_{n}}\right]+o(1).

Since
\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta})=O(n^{2\epsilon_{0}})
multiplying with the asymptotic equivalence rate
n^{-\kappa}
we have
n^{2\epsilon_{0}-\kappa}=o(1)
(we also choose
\epsilon_{0}<\kappa/2
). Finally we use relations (21) and (22) to obtain

E_{\rho_{\theta}}\left[E_{\rho_{\theta}}\left[\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta}_{n})|\mathds{1}_{A_{n}}\right]\right]\leq E_{\rho_{\theta}}\left[E_{\Phi^{\theta,r}}\left[\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta}_{n})|\mathds{1}_{A_{n}}\right]\right]+o(1).

Note that in this case
\Phi^{\theta,r}
depends on the center
\tilde{\rho}_{n}
(obtained in the first step). Using relation (
70
) we observe that
nP[A^{c}_{n}]=o(1)
and hence

E_{\rho}n||\rho-\hat{\rho}_{n}||_{2}^{2}\leq E_{\rho_{\theta}}\left[E_{\Phi^{\theta,r}}\left[\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta}_{n})|\mathds{1}_{A_{n}}\right]\right]+o(1).

(71)

Since we have used
\tilde{\rho}_{n}
as the centralizing state, if
\tilde{\mu}_{1}>\ldots>\tilde{\mu}_{r}

are the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\rho}_{n}
, then with
\theta\in\Theta
we have

E_{\Phi^{\theta,r}}\left[\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta}_{n})|\mathds{1}_{A_{n}}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\tilde{\mu}_{i}(1-\tilde{\mu}_{i})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}2\tilde{\mu}_{i}

by equation (
64
). Although we have used the second part of the sample
n_{2}
to form the estimate
\hat{\theta}_{n}
, since
n_{2}=n(1+o(1))
the same asymptotics hold.

Finally let
\sigma
be a rank-
r
qudit with eigenvalues
\mu_{1}>\ldots>\mu_{r}
, then if
||\rho-\sigma||_{2}<n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon<\epsilon_{0}
then
||\tilde{\rho}_{n}-\sigma||_{2}\leq n^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
with high probability (see RHS of (
69
)). By the equivalence of norms in the finite dimension the same claim can be made with the operator norm i.e.

P[||\sigma-\tilde{\rho}_{n}||\geq cn^{-1/2+\varepsilon}]\leq de^{-3n^{-1+2\epsilon_{0}+\delta_{0}}/400rd}

for some constant c. Using the above relation and Weyl perturbation inequality one can easily show that
\tilde{\mu}_{i}=\mu_{i}+o_{p}(1)
for all
i=1,\ldots,r-1
and since
\tilde{\mu}_{i}
are bounded we have

\limsup_{n}\sup_{||\rho-\sigma||_{2}<n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}}E_{\rho_{\theta}}\left[E_{\Phi^{\theta,r}}\left[\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta}_{n})|\mathds{1}_{A_{n}}\right]\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}(1-\mu_{i})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}2\mu_{i}.

(72)

Finally using relations (
71
) and (
72
) we obtain

\limsup_{n}\sup_{||\rho-\sigma||_{2}<n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}}E_{\rho}n||\rho-\hat{\rho}_{n}||_{2}^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}(1-\mu_{i})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}2\mu_{i}.

Lower Bound

Again we observe that for
||\rho-\sigma||<n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}
we can use the local parametrization and write
\rho
and
\hat{\rho}_{n}
as
\rho_{\theta}
and
\rho_{\theta_{n}}
respectively with the central state now being
\sigma
. We have the following set of inequalities

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\rho}_{n}}\sup_{||\rho-\sigma||<n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}}E_{\rho}n||\rho-\hat{\rho}_{n}||_{2}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\theta}_{n}}\sup_{||\theta||<n^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta})+O\left(\frac{||\theta||^{3},||\hat{\theta}_{n}||^{3}}{n^{1/2}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{||\hat{\theta}||<n^{\varepsilon}}\sup_{||\theta||<n^{\varepsilon}}E_{\rho_{\theta}}\left[\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta})+O\left(\frac{||\theta||^{3},||\hat{\theta}_{n}||^{3}}{n^{1/2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{||\hat{\theta}||<n^{\varepsilon}}\sup_{||\theta||<n^{\varepsilon}}E_{\rho_{\theta}}\left[\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta})\right]-o(1)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{||\hat{\theta}||<n^{\varepsilon}}\sup_{||\theta||<n^{\varepsilon}}E_{\Phi^{\theta,r}}\left[\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta})\right]-o(1)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n,\beta_{1},\gamma_{1}}}E_{\Phi^{\theta,r}}\left[\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta})\right]-o(1)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}(1-\mu_{i})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r\\
i<j\leq d\end{subarray}}2\mu_{i}-o(1).

The first inequality follows since
||\theta||<n^{\varepsilon}
implies
||\rho-\sigma||_{2}<n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}
. Since
||\theta||<n^{\varepsilon}
it is enough to consider
\hat{\theta}_{n}
with
||\hat{\theta}||_{n}<n^{\varepsilon}
hence the second inequality follows. For the third inequality we observe that one can choose
\varepsilon
small enough so that
3\varepsilon<1/2-\epsilon_{1}
for some
\epsilon_{1}
and hence for the given restriction of
\theta
and
\hat{\theta}_{n}
the remainder term is
o(1)
. Also for the same restriction of
\theta
and
\hat{\theta}_{n}
within the ball of radius
n^{\varepsilon}
, the supremum of the loss
\mathcal{L}(\theta,\hat{\theta}_{n})
is
O(n^{2\varepsilon})
. Let
\Theta_{n,\varepsilon}=\{\theta:||\theta||<n^{\varepsilon}\}
. Now fix
0<\beta<\gamma<1/9
which gives a rate of equivalence
n^{-\kappa}
for some
\kappa>0
(see Theorem 3.3). Choose
\varepsilon
small enough so that
n^{2\varepsilon-\kappa}=o(1)
and
\Theta_{n,\varepsilon}\subset\Theta_{n,r,\beta,\gamma}
so that we can use LAN and using (21) and (23) we obtain the fourth inequality. Now choose
\beta_{1}<\gamma_{1}<1/9
so that
\Theta_{n,r,\beta_{1},\gamma_{1}}\subset\Theta_{n,\varepsilon}
and the fifth inequality follows. We have also taken infimum over a larger class of estimators without increasing the maximum risk. We use lower bound from Theorem 5.2 to obtain the final inequality.
Finally taking limits we obtain

\liminf\inf_{\hat{\rho}_{n}}\sup_{||\rho-\sigma||_{2}<n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}}E_{\rho}n||\rho-\hat{\rho}_{n}||_{2}^{2}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}(1-\mu_{i})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i\leq r}\\
{i<j\leq d}\end{subarray}}2\mu_{i}.

∎

Proof of Theorem 6.1.

Upper Bound

Our aim is to show

\sup_{\rho\in\Sigma_{n,\varepsilon}\left(\rho_{0}\right)}E_{\rho}|n^{1/2}\left(\bar{X}_{n}-\Psi\left(\rho\right)\right)|^{2}\rightarrow V^{2}_{\rho_{0}}.

(73)

Let
Y_{1,n},\ldots,Y_{n,n}
be the array of independent r.v’s
all having the distribution of
n^{-1/2}\left(X_{A}-\Psi\left(\rho\right)\right)
under
\rho
;
we have to check

\displaystyle\sup_{\rho\in\Sigma_{n,\varepsilon}\left(\rho_{0}\right)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}EY_{n,i}^{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow V_{\rho_{0}}^{2}.

Since
Y_{1,n},\ldots,Y_{n,n}
have identical distributions, this is
equivalent, for a r.v.
Y_{n,0}=X_{A}-\Psi\left(\rho\right)
(distribution under
\rho
)

\displaystyle\sup_{\rho\in\Sigma_{n,\varepsilon}\left(\rho_{0}\right)}EY_{n,0}^{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow V_{\rho_{0}}^{2}.

(74)

Note that

EY_{n,0}^{2}=Var_{\rho}X_{A}=\mathrm{Tr}\;A^{2}\rho-\left(\mathrm{Tr}\;A\rho\right)^{2}.

Recall that
||A||
is the operator norm of
A
. We have the following inequalities

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Tr}\;A^{2}\rho-\mathrm{Tr}\;A^{2}\rho_{0}\right|

\displaystyle=\left\|A^{2}\right\|\left\|\rho-\rho_{0}\right\|_{1}\leq\left\|A^{2}\right\|\gamma_{n}=o\left(1\right)

where
\gamma_{n}=n^{-1/2+\varepsilon}
. Similarly we obtain

\left|\mathrm{Tr}\;A\rho-\mathrm{Tr}\;A\rho_{0}\right|\leq\left\|A\right\|\gamma_{n}=o\left(1\right).

The last two displays imply (
74
).

Lower Bound

We now have to establish the complement to
(
73
), namely

\liminf_{n}\inf_{\hat{\Psi}}\sup_{\rho\in\Sigma_{n,\varepsilon}\left(\rho_{0}\right)}E_{\rho}|n^{1/2}\left(\hat{\Psi}-\Psi\left(\rho\right)\right)|^{2}\geq V^{2}_{\rho_{0}}.

(75)

where
\inf_{\hat{\Psi}}
denotes the infimum over all measurements and all
estimators
\hat{\Psi}
.
We will construct, for a
d
-dimensional center state

\rho_{0}
(of rank
r
) and a parametrization of all
d
-dimensional nearby states, a "least favorable parametric subfamily" so that the
minimax risk of estimating
\Psi\left(\rho\right)
along this family will
be lower bounded by the minimax risk of estimating the parameter in the limiting model. Our perturbation
setup will be

\rho_{t}=\rho_{0}+n^{-1/2}tH\text{, \ \ }t\in\left(-n^{\varepsilon},n^{\varepsilon}\right)

(76)

where
H
is self-adjoint with
\mathrm{Tr}\;H=0
, and satisfies

\mathrm{Tr}\;AH=1.

(77)

Then we have

\displaystyle\Psi\left(\rho_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\;A\rho_{t}=\mathrm{Tr}\;A\rho_{0}+n^{-1/2}t

\displaystyle=\Psi\left(\rho_{0}\right)+n^{-1/2}t

so that
t
is the appropriate parameter, estimation of which is
equivalent to estimating the functional
\Psi
. Recall that
\left\langle A\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}=\mathrm{Tr}(\rho_{0}A)
for any operator
A
. For the correct choice of
H
,
set

\displaystyle\tilde{A}

\displaystyle:=A-\left\langle A\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{1,}

\displaystyle\tilde{H}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\tilde{A}\rho_{0}+\rho_{0}\tilde{A}\right),

\displaystyle\hat{H}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\tilde{H}}{\left\langle\tilde{A}^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}}.

(78)

With a choice
H=

\hat{H}
, the conditions on
H
are fulfilled:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\;\hat{H}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\tilde{A}^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}\;\tilde{H}=\left\langle A^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}\;\tilde{A}\rho_{0}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\tilde{A}^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}\left(A-\left\langle A\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{1}\right)\rho_{0}

\displaystyle=0

and for (
77
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\;A\hat{H}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\;\left(A-\left\langle A\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}\mathbf{1}\right)\hat{H}=\mathrm{Tr}\;\tilde{A}\hat{H}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\tilde{A}^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}\;\tilde{A}\tilde{H}=\left\langle\tilde{A}^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}\;\tilde{A}^{2}\rho_{0}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\tilde{A}^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}^{-1}\left\langle\tilde{A}^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}=1.

We also note that

\displaystyle V_{\rho_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Var}_{\rho_{0}}\left(X_{A}\right)

\displaystyle=E_{\rho_{0}}\left(X_{A}-E_{\rho_{0}}X_{A}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}\;\tilde{A}^{2}\rho_{0}=\left\langle\tilde{A}^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho_{0}}.

We now proceed to find the limiting classical-quantum Gaussian model for this choice of perturbation. It can be easily verified that up to first order terms
\rho_{\vartheta,r}
is equal to
\tilde{\rho}_{\vartheta,r}
where
\tilde{\rho}_{\vartheta,r}
given as follows

\tilde{\rho}_{\vartheta/\sqrt{n},r}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mu_{1}+\tilde{u}_{1}&\tilde{\zeta}_{1,2}^{*}&\dots&\tilde{\zeta}_{1,r}^{*}&\vline&\tilde{\zeta}^{*}_{1,r+1}&\tilde{\zeta}_{1,r+2}&\dots&\tilde{\zeta}^{*}_{1,d}\\
\tilde{\zeta}_{1,2}&\mu_{2}+\tilde{u}_{2}&\ddots&\vdots&\vline&\tilde{\zeta}_{2,r+1}&\tilde{\zeta}^{*}_{2,r+2}&\ddots&\vdots\par\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\tilde{\zeta}_{r-1,r}^{*}&\vline&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\tilde{\zeta}^{*}_{r-1,d}\\
\tilde{\zeta}_{1,r}&\dots&\tilde{\zeta}_{r-1,r}&\mu_{r}-\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}\tilde{u}_{i}&\vline&\tilde{\zeta}^{*}_{r,r+1}&\dots&\tilde{\zeta}^{*}_{r,d-1}&\tilde{\zeta}^{*}_{r,d}\\
\hline\cr\tilde{\zeta}_{1,r+1}&\tilde{\zeta}_{2,r+1}&\dots&\tilde{\zeta}_{r,r+1}&\vline&0&0&\dots&0\\
\tilde{\zeta}_{1,r+2}&\tilde{\zeta}_{2,r+2}&\ddots&\vdots&\vline&0&0&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\tilde{\zeta}_{r,d-1}&\vline&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
\tilde{\zeta}_{1,d}&\dots&\tilde{\zeta}_{r-1,d}&\tilde{\zeta}_{r,d}&\vline&0&\dots&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}

(79)

where
\tilde{u}=n^{-1/2}u,\penalty\ \tilde{\zeta}=n^{-1/2}\zeta
and
u_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\penalty\ \zeta_{j,k}\in\mathbb{C}
. From (
76
) we obtain

n^{-1/2}t\hat{H}=n^{-1/2}\begin{bmatrix}u_{1}&\zeta_{1,2}^{*}&\dots&\zeta_{1,r}^{*}&\vline&\zeta^{*}_{1,r+1}&\zeta_{1,r+2}&\dots&\zeta^{*}_{1,d}\\
\zeta_{1,2}&u_{2}&\ddots&\vdots&\vline&\zeta^{*}_{2,r+1}&\zeta^{*}_{2,r+2}&\ddots&\vdots\par\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\zeta_{r-1,r}^{*}&\vline&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\zeta^{*}_{r-1,d}\\
\zeta_{1,r}&\dots&\zeta_{r-1,r}&-\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}u_{i}&\vline&\zeta^{*}_{r,r+1}&\dots&\zeta^{*}_{r,d-1}&\zeta^{*}_{r,d}\\
\hline\cr\zeta_{1,r+1}&\zeta_{2,r+1}&\dots&\zeta_{r,r+1}&\vline&0&0&\dots&0\\
\zeta_{1,r+2}&\zeta_{2,r+2}&\ddots&\vdots&\vline&0&0&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\zeta_{r,d-1}&\vline&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
\zeta_{1,d}&\dots&\zeta_{r-1,d}&\zeta_{r,d}&\vline&0&\dots&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}.

(80)

Let
x=\langle A\rangle_{\rho_{0}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}A_{ii}
and
y=\langle\tilde{A}^{2}\rangle_{\rho_{0}}
.
It can be easily verified that
